 &=-\frac1n\ResidueOperator_w H(w)(f(w)^{-n})'\Differential w\\
 &=\frac1n\ResidueOperator_w H'(w)f(w)^{-n}\Differential w,
\end{align*}
where the residue of a derivative is zero.  Since
$f(w)^{-n}=w^{-n}\phi(w)^n$, this is
\eqref{eq:lagrange-burmann}.  Applying the same zero-residue rule to
$H(w)\phi(w)^nw^{-n}$ gives
\[
 \frac1n\ResidueOperator H'\phi^nw^{-n}
 =\ResidueOperator H\phi^nw^{-n-1}-\ResidueOperator H\phi^{n-1}\phi'w^{-n},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:lagrange-burmann-alt}.  Take $H(w)=w$ for
\eqref{eq:lagrange-basic}.
\end{proof}

\section{Analytic inverse functions}
\begin{theorem}[Analytic Lagrange inversion]\label{thm:analytic-lagrange}
Let $f$ be analytic near $a$, let $b=f(a)$, and suppose $f'(a)\ne0$.  Its local
inverse $g$ is analytic near $b$ and has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:analytic-lagrange-series}
 g(z)=a+\sum_{n\ge1}g_n\frac{(z-b)^n}{n!},
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:analytic-lagrange-coeff}
 g_n=\left.\frac{\Differential^{n-1}}{\Differential w^{n-1}}
 \left(\frac{w-a}{f(w)-f(a)}\right)^n\right|_{w=a}.
\end{equation}
The series has a positive radius of convergence.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The analytic inverse-function theorem gives an analytic local inverse and therefore
a convergent Taylor series.  Put $u=w-a$, $\LagrangeShiftCoordinate=z-b$, and
\[
 \phi(u)=\frac{u}{f(a+u)-f(a)};
\]
its constant term is $1/f'(a)$.  The inverse displacement satisfies
$u=\LagrangeShiftCoordinate\phi(u)$.  Formula \eqref{eq:lagrange-basic}, multiplied by $n!$, is
exactly \eqref{eq:analytic-lagrange-coeff}.
\end{proof}

If $f'(a)=0$ but $f(w)-f(a)=c(w-a)^q+O((w-a)^{q+1})$ with $c\ne0$, factor
$f(w)-f(a)=(w-a)^qU(w)$ with $U(a)=c$.  Choosing one of the $q$ values of
$c^{-1/q}$ reduces the equation to ordinary inversion in the variable
$(z-f(a))^{1/q}$.  Thus the inverse has $q$ local Puiseux branches.  This is the
precise sense in which Lagrange inversion extends to a critical point; a
single-valued Taylor inverse cannot exist there.

\section{Inverse coefficients in Bell-polynomial form}
Suppose
\[
 f(w)=\sum_{j\ge1}f_j\frac{w^j}{j!},\qquad f_1\ne0,
 \qquad
 g(z)=f^{\langle-1\rangle}(z)=\sum_{n\ge1}g_n\frac{z^n}{n!}.
\]
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fhat}
 \NormalizedReversionCoefficient{f}{j}=\frac{f_{j+1}}{(j+1)f_1}.
\end{equation}
\begin{theorem}[Explicit inverse coefficients]\label{thm:inverse-bell-coeff}
One has $g_1=f_1^{-1}$ and, for $n\ge2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inverse-bell-coeff}
 g_n=\frac1{f_1^n}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(-1)^k\RisingFactorial nk
 \ExponentialPartialBellPolynomial{n-1}{k}(\NormalizedReversionCoefficient{f}{1},\ldots,\NormalizedReversionCoefficient{f}{n-k}).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write
\[
 \frac{f(w)}{f_1w}=1+U(w),
 \qquad U(w)=\sum_{j\ge1}\NormalizedReversionCoefficient{f}{j}\frac{w^j}{j!}.
\]
By \eqref{eq:lagrange-basic},
$g_n=(n-1)!\CoefficientExtraction w{n-1}(w/f(w))^n$.  Now
\[
 (1+U)^{-n}=\sum_{k\ge0}(-1)^k\binom{n+k-1}{k}U^k
 =\sum_{k\ge0}(-1)^k\frac{\RisingFactorial nk}{k!}U^k.
\]
Apply \eqref{eq:partial-bell-egf} and extract degree $n-1$; the $k=0$ term
vanishes when $n\ge2$.
\end{proof}

\section{The associahedron interpretation, with normalized notation}
The formula in the archive mixes exponential coefficients with an ordinary-series
face formula.  The exact geometric statement is the following.

Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ordinary-series-associa}
 A(x)=x+\sum_{n\ge1}a_nx^{n+1},\qquad
 B(x)=A^{\langle-1\rangle}(x)=x+\sum_{n\ge1}b_nx^{n+1}.
\end{equation}
Use the indexing in which the associahedron $\AssociahedronByDimension{n-1}$ has dimension $n-1$ and its
faces are plane rooted trees with $n+1$ leaves and internal arities at least $2$.
If an internal vertex has arity $i+1$, give it weight $a_i$; let $a_F$ be the
product of all vertex weights.  Equivalently, if
$F\cong \AssociahedronByDimension{i_1-1}\times\cdots\times
\AssociahedronByDimension{i_m-1}$, then
$a_F=a_{i_1}\cdots a_{i_m}$.

\begin{theorem}[Associahedral inversion]\label{thm:associahedral-inversion}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:associahedral-inversion}
 b_n=\sum_{F\text{ a face of }\AssociahedronByDimension{n-1}}(-1)^{n-\dim F}a_F.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The equation $A(B)=x$ is
\[
 B=x-\sum_{i\ge1}a_iB^{i+1}.
\]
Iterating it recursively expands $b_n$ as a sum over plane rooted trees with
$n+1$ leaves: every internal vertex of arity $i+1$ contributes $-a_i$.
A face $F$ of $\AssociahedronByDimension{n-1}$ is exactly such a tree after contracting a chosen set of
internal edges.  If it has $m$ internal vertices, then
$\dim F=n-m$, so its sign is $(-1)^m=(-1)^{n-\dim F}$ and its weight is $a_F$.
Thus the tree expansion is the alternating face sum.
\end{proof}

For example,
\begin{align*}
 b_1&=-a_1,\\
 b_2&=-a_2+2a_1^2,\\
 b_3&=-a_3+5a_2a_1-5a_1^3,\\
 b_4&=-a_4+6a_3a_1+3a_2^2-21a_2a_1^2+14a_1^4.
\end{align*}
For $\AssociahedronByDimension3$, the coefficients $1,6,3,21,14$ count respectively the whole
3-polytope, its six pentagonal and three square facets, its edges, and its
vertices.

\chapter{Algebraic and transcendental examples}

\section{The Fuss--Catalan algebraic equation}
Let $p\ge2$ be an integer and consider
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fuss-equation}
 x^p-x+z=0,
 \qquad x(0)=0.
\end{equation}
It is equivalent to
$x=z\phi(x)$ with $\phi(x)=(1-x^{p-1})^{-1}$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:fuss-series}
The distinguished solution is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fuss-series}
 x(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{pk}{k}
       \frac{z^{(p-1)k+1}}{(p-1)k+1}.
\end{equation}
Its radius of convergence is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fuss-radius}
 R_p=(p-1)p^{-p/(p-1)},
\end{equation}
and the series converges absolutely also on $|z|=R_p$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq:lagrange-basic},
\[
 \CoefficientExtraction znx(z)=\frac1n\CoefficientExtraction w{n-1}(1-w^{p-1})^{-n}.
\]
This vanishes unless $n-1=(p-1)k$.  In that case the coefficient is
$\frac1n\binom{n+k-1}{k}=\binom{pk}{k}/((p-1)k+1)$, proving the series.

The inverse map is $z=f(x)=x-x^p$.  Its nearest critical points satisfy
$f'(x)=1-px^{p-1}=0$ and all have modulus $p^{-1/(p-1)}$; their critical values
have modulus $(p-1)p^{-p/(p-1)}$.  No local inverse can pass analytically through
a critical value, while the inverse-function theorem continues the branch up to
one, so this is the radius.  Stirling's formula gives coefficients of order
$Ck^{-3/2}R_p^{-((p-1)k+1)}$, which proves absolute convergence on the boundary.
\end{proof}

\section{Lambert's \texorpdfstring{$W$}{W} function at the origin}
The Lambert function satisfies $W(z)e^{W(z)}=z$.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:lambert-W-zero}
On its principal branch near $0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lambert-W-zero}
 W(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-n)^{n-1}\frac{z^n}{n!}
 =z-z^2+\frac32z^3-\frac83z^4+O(z^5),
\end{equation}
with radius of convergence $e^{-1}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Here $f(w)=we^w$, so $w/f(w)=e^{-w}$.  Formula
\eqref{eq:analytic-lagrange-coeff} gives
$g_n=D^{n-1}e^{-nw}|_{w=0}=(-n)^{n-1}$.  The inverse map has its nearest critical
point at $w=-1$, where $f(-1)=-e^{-1}$; this square-root branch point determines
the radius.
\end{proof}

\section{A rapidly useful expansion around \texorpdfstring{$W(e)=1$}{W(e)=1}}
Put $\xi=\log z-1$ and $u=W(z)-1$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lambert-near-e-implicit}
 u+\log(1+u)=\xi.
\end{equation}
Reversion gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lambert-near-e}
 W(e^{1+\xi})=1+\frac{\xi}{2}+\frac{\xi^2}{16}
 -\frac{\xi^3}{192}-\frac{\xi^4}{3072}
 +\frac{13\xi^5}{61440}+O(\xi^6).
\end{equation}
\begin{proof}
The derivative at $u=0$ of the left side of
\eqref{eq:lambert-near-e-implicit} is $2$, so it has an ordinary inverse.
Substitute $u=c_1\xi+\cdots+c_5\xi^5$ into
$u+\log(1+u)=\xi$ and compare successive powers.  The triangular equations give
$c_1=1/2,c_2=1/16,c_3=-1/192,c_4=-1/3072,c_5=13/61440$.
\end{proof}
The closest critical points of $u+\log(1+u)$ satisfy
$1+(1+u)^{-1}=0$, hence $u=-2$; using the two adjacent logarithm values gives
images $-2\pm i\pi$.  Therefore the radius in the $\xi$-plane is
$\sqrt{4+\pi^2}$.  For positive real $z$, the expansion converges whenever
$|\log z-1|<\sqrt{4+\pi^2}$, approximately
$0.0655<z<112.63$.  Taking $\xi=-1$ yields a convergent evaluation of
$W(1)=0.567143\ldots$.

\section{Binary trees and Catalan numbers}
Let $\CatalanNumber n$ count rooted ordered full binary trees with $n$ internal vertices, and
let $B(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}\CatalanNumber n z^n$.  Removing the root gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:binary-tree-functional}
 B(z)=1+zB(z)^2.
\end{equation}
Put $C(z)=B(z)-1$; then $C=z(1+C)^2$.  Lagrange inversion gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:catalan-lagrange}
 \CatalanNumber n=\CoefficientExtraction znC(z)=\frac1n\CoefficientExtraction w{n-1}(1+w)^{2n}
 =\frac1n\binom{2n}{n-1}=\frac1{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}.
\end{equation}
This simultaneously proves the functional equation, its unique formal solution,
and the Catalan closed form.  The semantic label $\CatalanNumber n$ avoids confusing
these binary-tree numbers with Bell numbers.

\chapter{Lagrange's reversion theorem in shifted form}

\section{The two-parameter implicit equation}
Let $v=v(x,y)$ be the unique formal solution of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shifted-reversion-equation}
 v=x+yf(v),
 \qquad v(x,0)=x.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Lagrange reversion]\label{thm:shifted-lagrange-reversion}
For every formal or analytic $g$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shifted-lagrange-reversion}
 g(v)=g(x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{y^k}{k!}
 \left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\right)^{k-1}
 \bigl(f(x)^kg'(x)\bigr).
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shifted-lagrange-v}
 v=x+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{y^k}{k!}
 \left(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\right)^{k-1}f(x)^k.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $u=v-x$.  Then $u=y\phi(u)$ with $\phi(u)=f(x+u)$, treating $x$ as a
coefficient parameter.  Apply \eqref{eq:lagrange-burmann} to
$H(u)=g(x+u)$:
\[
 [y^k]g(v)=\frac1k[u^{k-1}]g'(x+u)f(x+u)^k
 =\frac1{k!}D_x^{k-1}(g'(x)f(x)^k).
\]
Summation proves the first formula; take $g(z)=z$ for the second.
\end{proof}

\section{The delta-function proof decoded}
The archive also gives a distributional derivation.  Assume temporarily that
$x,y\in\RealNumbers$, the equation $z-x-yf(z)=0$ has one simple root $v$, and
$1-yf'(v)>0$.  The elementary change-of-variables rule for the Dirac delta gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:delta-root}
 g(v)=\int_{\RealNumbers}\delta(yf(z)-z+x)g(z)(1-yf'(z))\,\Differential z.
\end{equation}
Using
$\delta(s)=\int_{\RealNumbers}e^{iks}\Differential k/(2\pi)$ and expanding only in the formal
parameter $y$,
\begin{align}
 g(v)
 &=\sum_{n\ge0}D_x^n\left[
 \frac{y^nf(x)^ng(x)}{n!}(1-yf'(x))\right]\notag\\
 &=\sum_{n\ge0}D_x^n\left[
 \frac{y^nf(x)^ng(x)}{n!}
 -\frac{y^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\bigl((gf^{n+1})'-g'f^{n+1}\bigr)
 \right].
 \label{eq:delta-reversion-middle}
\end{align}
The first term of level $n+1$ cancels the total derivative in the second term of
level $n$; what remains is exactly
\eqref{eq:shifted-lagrange-reversion}.  Thus the Fourier--delta calculation is a
compact analytic avatar of the formal residue proof.  Formula
\eqref{eq:delta-root} requires an absolute Jacobian in general; the displayed sign
is valid in the stated orientation-preserving neighbourhood.

\chapter{Laplace-type asymptotic integrals}
\index{Laplace asymptotics}

\section{Endpoint expansion}
Consider
\begin{equation}\label{eq:laplace-integral}
 I(\lambda)=\int_a^b e^{-\lambda f(x)}g(x)\,\Differential x,
 \qquad \lambda\to+\infty,
\end{equation}
where the endpoint $a$ is the unique relevant minimum and, with $t=x-a$,
\begin{align}
 f(a+t)&\sim f(a)+\sum_{k\ge0}a_kt^{k+\alpha},
 &a_0>0,\quad\alpha>0,
 \label{eq:laplace-f-expansion}\\
 g(a+t)&\sim\sum_{k\ge0}b_kt^{k+\beta-1},
 &\beta>0.
 \label{eq:laplace-g-expansion}
\end{align}
Let $\OrdinaryPartialBellPolynomial{k}{j}$ denote the ordinary Bell polynomial.

\begin{theorem}[Laplace--Erd\'elyi coefficient formula]
\label{thm:laplace-bell}
Under the standard differentiability and remainder hypotheses that permit Watson's
lemma,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:laplace-asymptotic}
 I(\lambda)\sim e^{-\lambda f(a)}
 \sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
 \Gamma\!\left(\frac{n+\beta}{\alpha}\right)
 \frac{c_n}{\lambda^{(n+\beta)/\alpha}},
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:laplace-cn}
 c_n=\frac1{\alpha a_0^{(n+\beta)/\alpha}}
 \sum_{k=0}^{n}b_{n-k}
 \sum_{j=0}^{k}
 \binom{-\frac{n+\beta}{\alpha}}{j}
 \frac{\OrdinaryPartialBellPolynomial{k}{j}(a_1,\ldots,a_{k-j+1})}{a_0^j}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write
$f(a+t)-f(a)=t^\alpha A(t)$ with
$A(t)=a_0+a_1t+\cdots$, and set
$s=(f(a+t)-f(a))^{1/\alpha}=tA(t)^{1/\alpha}$.  This has an inverse $t=t(s)$.
Expand
\[
 g(a+t(s))t'(s)=\sum_{n\ge0}d_ns^{n+\beta-1}.
\]
A generalized Lagrange--Bürmann calculation, first for integral $\beta$ and then
by formal continuation in the exponent, gives
\[
 d_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n}b_{n-k}[t^k]A(t)^{-(n+\beta)/\alpha}.
\]
Indeed, apply \eqref{eq:lagrange-burmann} to an antiderivative of
$t^{m+\beta-1}$ and differentiate back after substitution.

Put $\rho=(n+\beta)/\alpha$.  The ordinary Bell expansion yields
\begin{align*}
 [t^k]A(t)^{-\rho}
 &=a_0^{-\rho}[t^k]
 \sum_{j\ge0}\binom{-\rho}{j}
 \left(\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{a_r}{a_0}t^r\right)^j\\
 &=a_0^{-\rho}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{-\rho}{j}a_0^{-j}
 \OrdinaryPartialBellPolynomial{k}{j}(a_1,\ldots,a_{k-j+1}).
\end{align*}
Thus $c_n=d_n/\alpha$ is \eqref{eq:laplace-cn}.  Finally
\[
 \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda s^\alpha}s^{n+\beta-1}\Differential s
 =\frac1\alpha\Gamma\!\left(\frac{n+\beta}{\alpha}\right)
 \lambda^{-(n+\beta)/\alpha},
\]
and Watson's lemma controls the replacement of the finite endpoint interval by
the asymptotic expansion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
The appearance of ordinary rather than exponential Bell polynomials is forced by
the ordinary Taylor expansion of $A(t)^{-\rho}$.  Lagrange inversion enters in the
change from $t$ to the natural Laplace coordinate $s$; the gamma factor enters only
in the final monomial integral.
\end{remark}

% -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
% New material synthesized from the five drafts, the supplied Wikipedia and
% MathWorld reference folders, and the external primary/authoritative sources.
% -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

\part{Higher Composition, Umbral Calculus, and Multivariate Inversion}

\chapter{Products, inverse derivatives, and Fr\'echet composition}
\label{ch:higher-composition}

The one-variable Fa\`a di Bruno formula already proved in this monograph is the
central composition theorem, but several useful consequences were distributed
among the five drafts.  This chapter gathers them in a normalization that is
compatible with the Bell-polynomial notation used throughout.  It also explains
why set partitions continue to be the correct indexing objects in Banach spaces,
where there is no preferred coordinate system.

\section{The multinomial Leibniz rule}

\begin{theorem}[Leibniz rule for many factors]
Let $f_1,\ldots,f_q$ be $n$ times differentiable.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\Differential^n}{\Differential x^n}\prod_{r=1}^q f_r(x)
 =\sum_{j_1+\cdots+j_q=n}
   \binom{n}{j_1,\ldots,j_q}
   \prod_{r=1}^q f_r^{(j_r)}(x).
 \label{new:eq:multinomial-leibniz}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Introduce a formal increment $h$.  Taylor's formula, interpreted formally when
only derivatives at $x$ are needed, gives
\[
 f_r(x+h)=\sum_{j\ge0}f_r^{(j)}(x)\frac{h^j}{j!}.
\]
Multiplying these $q$ series and extracting the coefficient of $h^n$ yields
\[
 [h^n]\prod_{r=1}^q f_r(x+h)
 =\sum_{j_1+\cdots+j_q=n}
   \prod_{r=1}^q\frac{f_r^{(j_r)}(x)}{j_r!}.
\]
The left side is $(\prod_r f_r)^{(n)}(x)/n!$.  Multiplication by $n!$ gives
\eqref{new:eq:multinomial-leibniz}.
\end{proof}

The proof shows more than the formula: the multinomial coefficient is not an
extra combinatorial decoration.  It is exactly the number of ways to distribute
$n$ labelled differentiations among $q$ factors with occupation numbers
$j_1,\ldots,j_q$.

\section{Reciprocals and higher quotient rules}

\begin{proposition}[Derivatives of a reciprocal]
If $g(x)\ne0$, then for $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \left(\frac1g\right)^{(n)}
 =\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k k!\,
   \frac{\ExponentialPartialBellPolynomial nk(g',g'',\ldots,g^{(n-k+1)})}{g^{k+1}}.
 \label{new:eq:reciprocal-bell}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 \left(\frac fg\right)^{(n)}
 =\sum_{r=0}^n\binom nr f^{(n-r)}
   \sum_{k=0}^{r}(-1)^k k!\,
   \frac{\ExponentialPartialBellPolynomial rk(g',g'',\ldots,g^{(r-k+1)})}{g^{k+1}}.
 \label{new:eq:quotient-bell}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Apply Fa\`a di Bruno to $u\mapsto u^{-1}$ composed with $g$.  Its $k$th
derivative is $(-1)^k k!u^{-k-1}$, which gives
\eqref{new:eq:reciprocal-bell}.  Apply the ordinary two-factor Leibniz rule to
$f\,g^{-1}$ and substitute the reciprocal formula to obtain
\eqref{new:eq:quotient-bell}.
\end{proof}

\section{Inverse-function derivatives}

Let $y=f(x)$, suppose $f'(x)\ne0$, and write $x=g(y)=f^{-1}(y)$.  The simplest
closed description of all derivatives of $g$ is an operator identity.

\begin{theorem}[Inverse-derivative operator]
For $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 g^{(n)}(y)
 =\left.
   \left(\frac1{f'(x)}\frac{\Differential}{\Differential x}\right)^{n-1}
   \frac1{f'(x)}\right|_{x=g(y)}.
 \label{new:eq:inverse-operator}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for $n\ge2$,
\begin{equation}
 g^{(n)}(y)=-\frac1{f'(g(y))}
 \sum_{k=2}^{n} f^{(k)}(g(y))
 \ExponentialPartialBellPolynomial nk\bigl(g'(y),g''(y),\ldots,g^{(n-k+1)}(y)\bigr).
 \label{new:eq:inverse-recursion}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $x=g(y)$ and $\Differential y/\Differential x=f'(x)$, differentiation along the inverse
curve satisfies
\[
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential y}=\frac1{f'(x)}\frac{\Differential}{\Differential x}.
\]
Repeated application, starting from $g'(y)=1/f'(x)$, proves
\eqref{new:eq:inverse-operator}.  For the recursion, differentiate the identity
$f(g(y))=y$ exactly $n$ times.  Fa\`a di Bruno gives
\[
 \sum_{k=1}^n f^{(k)}(g(y))
 \ExponentialPartialBellPolynomial nk(g',g'',\ldots)=0
 \qquad(n\ge2).
\]
The $k=1$ term is $f'(g(y))g^{(n)}(y)$.  Isolating it gives
\eqref{new:eq:inverse-recursion}.
\end{proof}

The first four derivatives, with every derivative of $f$ evaluated at
$x=g(y)$, are
\begin{align*}
 g'&=\frac1{f'},\\
 g''&=-\frac{f''}{(f')^3},\\
 g'''&=\frac{3(f'')^2-f'f'''}{(f')^5},\\
 g^{(4)}&=\frac{-15(f'')^3+10f'f''f'''-(f')^2f^{(4)}}{(f')^7}.
\end{align*}
These expressions provide useful independent checks on coefficients obtained
from Lagrange inversion.

\section{Coordinate-free Fa\`a di Bruno}

Let $E,F,G$ be Banach spaces.  For a sufficiently differentiable map, $D^r$
denotes its symmetric $r$-linear Fr\'echet derivative.  If $B\subseteq[n]$,
write $h_B$ for the ordered list $(h_i)_{i\in B}$; symmetry makes the order
irrelevant.

\begin{theorem}[Fr\'echet Fa\`a di Bruno formula]
Let $g:E\to F$ and $f:F\to G$ be $n$ times Fr\'echet differentiable.  Then
\begin{equation}
 D^n(f\circ g)(x)[h_1,\ldots,h_n]
 =\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_n}
 D^{|\pi|}f(g(x))
 \Bigl[\,D^{|B|}g(x)[h_B] \,\Bigr]_{B\in\pi},
 \label{new:eq:frechet-faa}
\end{equation}
where $\Pi_n$ is the set of set partitions of $[n]$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The assertion for $n=1$ is the chain rule.  Assume it for $n$ and differentiate
in a new direction $h_{n+1}$.  In a term indexed by $\pi\in\Pi_n$, the new
derivative can act in exactly two ways.

If it acts on the outer derivative, the chain rule inserts
$Dg(x)[h_{n+1}]$ as a new argument of $D^{|\pi|+1}f$.  Combinatorially this adds
the singleton block $\{n+1\}$ to $\pi$.  If it acts on the factor belonging to
a block $B$, it changes
$D^{|B|}g(x)[h_B]$ to
$D^{|B|+1}g(x)[h_B,h_{n+1}]$; combinatorially this adjoins $n+1$ to $B$.
Every partition of $[n+1]$ is obtained uniquely in one of these ways: remove
$n+1$ from its block, and distinguish whether the block becomes empty.  Hence
the differentiated sum is exactly the sum over $\Pi_{n+1}$.
\end{proof}

When $E=F=G=\RealNumbers$ and all directions equal $1$, grouping partitions by their
block-size multiplicities reproduces the usual Bell-polynomial form.  Thus the
coordinate-free theorem is not a different formula; it is the invariant source
of the scalar one.

\section{Nested composition and partition hierarchies}

For three scalar functions, one explicit formula is obtained by substituting
\[
 (g\circ h)^{(j)}
 =\sum_{\ell=0}^j g^{(\ell)}(h(x))
   \ExponentialPartialBellPolynomial j\ell(h',h'',\ldots)
\]
into
\[
 D^n f(g(h(x)))
 =\sum_{k=0}^n f^{(k)}(g(h(x)))
   \ExponentialPartialBellPolynomial nk\bigl((g\circ h)',(g\circ h)'',\ldots\bigr).
\]
The resulting finite expression is closed, but its structure is clearer as a
hierarchy of partitions.  An inner partition groups derivative labels that hit
the same occurrence of $h$; an outer partition groups the resulting inner
blocks according to occurrences of $g$.  Flattening this hierarchy is the
combinatorial shadow of associativity of formal-series composition.

\chapter{Partition lattices, cumulants, and M\"obius inversion}
\label{ch:partition-lattice}

Bell polynomials encode the forward passage from connected components to
arbitrary assemblies.  The inverse passage is M\"obius inversion on the lattice
of set partitions.  Making that lattice explicit simultaneously proves the
moment--cumulant formulas, explains the logarithm of an exponential generating
function, and supplies a reusable inversion principle.

\section{The partition lattice}

Let $\Pi_n$ be ordered by refinement: $\sigma\le\pi$ when every block of
$\sigma$ lies inside a block of $\pi$.  Its minimum is the discrete partition
$\PartitionLatticeMinimum$ and its maximum is the one-block partition $\PartitionLatticeMaximum$.

\begin{theorem}[M\"obius function of the partition lattice]
For $\pi\in\Pi_n$,
\begin{equation}
 \mu(\PartitionLatticeMinimum,\pi)
 =(-1)^{n-|\pi|}\prod_{B\in\pi}(|B|-1)!.
 \label{new:eq:partition-mobius}
\end{equation}
More generally, if $\sigma\le\pi$ and $m_B$ is the number of blocks of
$\sigma$ contained in $B\in\pi$, then
\begin{equation}
 \mu(\sigma,\pi)
 =\prod_{B\in\pi}(-1)^{m_B-1}(m_B-1)!.
 \label{new:eq:interval-mobius}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The interval $[\sigma,\pi]$ is the direct product, over $B\in\pi$, of partition
lattices on the $m_B$ blocks of $\sigma$ lying inside $B$.  The M\"obius
function is multiplicative on direct products, so it is enough to prove
\[
 \mu_{\Pi_m}(\PartitionLatticeMinimum,\PartitionLatticeMaximum)=(-1)^{m-1}(m-1)!.
\]
Let $a_m$ denote this value.  The defining M\"obius relation says, for $m>1$,
\[
 0=\sum_{\rho\in\Pi_m}\mu(\PartitionLatticeMinimum,\rho)
  =\sum_{\rho\in\Pi_m}\prod_{C\in\rho}a_{|C|}.
\]
The exponential formula therefore gives
\[
 1+\sum_{m\ge1}\left(\sum_{\rho\in\Pi_m}
       \prod_{C\in\rho}a_{|C|}\right)\frac{z^m}{m!}
 =\exp\left(\sum_{m\ge1}a_m\frac{z^m}{m!}\right).
\]
The inner sum equals $1$ for $m=1$ and $0$ for $m>1$, so the left side is
$1+z$.  Taking logarithms gives
\[
 \sum_{m\ge1}a_m\frac{z^m}{m!}=\log(1+z)
 =\sum_{m\ge1}(-1)^{m-1}\frac{z^m}{m}.
\]
Hence $a_m=(-1)^{m-1}(m-1)!$.  Multiplicativity gives
\eqref{new:eq:interval-mobius}, and the special case $\sigma=\PartitionLatticeMinimum$ gives
\eqref{new:eq:partition-mobius}.
\end{proof}

\section{Moments and cumulants}

Let $m_n$ and $\kappa_n$ be two sequences with $m_0=1$.  Think of them as
moments and cumulants, but the identities are purely algebraic.

\begin{theorem}[Moment--cumulant inversion]
The following statements are equivalent:
\begin{align}
 m_n&=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_n}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|},
 \label{new:eq:moment-from-cumulant}\\
 \kappa_n&=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_n}
 (-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!
 \prod_{B\in\pi}m_{|B|},
 \label{new:eq:cumulant-from-moment}\\
 M(z)&=\exp K(z),
 \quad
 M(z)=1+\sum_{n\ge1}m_n\frac{z^n}{n!},
 \quad
 K(z)=\sum_{n\ge1}\kappa_n\frac{z^n}{n!}.
 \label{new:eq:moment-cumulant-egf}
\end{align}
Equivalently, $m_n=\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial n(\kappa_1,\ldots,\kappa_n)$ and
$\kappa_n$ is the logarithmic Bell transform of the moments.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The exponential formula immediately makes
\eqref{new:eq:moment-from-cumulant} equivalent to
\eqref{new:eq:moment-cumulant-egf}.  To invert the partition sum, define on
$\Pi_n$
\[
 F(\pi)=\prod_{B\in\pi}m_{|B|},\qquad
 G(\pi)=\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}.
\]
Equation \eqref{new:eq:moment-from-cumulant}, applied independently inside each
block of $\pi$, is $F(\pi)=\sum_{\sigma\le\pi}G(\sigma)$.  M\"obius inversion
and \eqref{new:eq:interval-mobius} give
\[
 G(\PartitionLatticeMaximum)=\sum_{\pi\le\PartitionLatticeMaximum}\mu(\pi,\PartitionLatticeMaximum)F(\pi)
 =\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_n}(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!
  \prod_{B\in\pi}m_{|B|},
\]
which is \eqref{new:eq:cumulant-from-moment}.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[The first cumulants]
The inversion gives
\begin{align*}
 \kappa_1&=m_1,\\
 \kappa_2&=m_2-m_1^2,\\
 \kappa_3&=m_3-3m_2m_1+2m_1^3,\\
 \kappa_4&=m_4-4m_3m_1-3m_2^2+12m_2m_1^2-6m_1^4.
\end{align*}
For a random variable these are, respectively, the mean, variance, third
central moment, and fourth central moment minus three times the variance
squared.
\end{example}

\chapter{Binomial type, Sheffer sequences, and exponential Riordan arrays}
\label{ch:sheffer-riordan}

The Stirling matrices, Bell transforms, and many polynomial families in the
preceding chapters belong to one algebraic group.  The appropriate language is
that of Sheffer sequences and exponential Riordan arrays.  This chapter proves
the structural results directly from generating functions, so no umbral
formalism is required as a prerequisite.

\section{Polynomial sequences of binomial type}

A polynomial sequence $(q_n(x))_{n\ge0}$, with $\deg q_n=n$ and $q_0=1$, is of
\emph{binomial type} if
\begin{equation}
 q_n(x+y)=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk q_k(x)q_{n-k}(y).
 \label{new:eq:binomial-type}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Generating-function characterization]
A polynomial sequence is of binomial type if and only if there is a delta
series $B(t)=b_1t+b_2t^2+\cdots$, $b_1\ne0$, such that
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n\ge0}q_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}=\exp\bigl(xB(t)\bigr).
 \label{new:eq:binomial-type-egf}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
If \eqref{new:eq:binomial-type-egf} holds, multiplying the generating functions
at $x$ and $y$ gives the one at $x+y$; coefficient extraction is exactly
\eqref{new:eq:binomial-type}.

Conversely, put $Q(x,t)=\sum q_n(x)t^n/n!$.  The binomial identity says
$Q(x+y,t)=Q(x,t)Q(y,t)$ and $Q(0,t)=1$.  Formal differentiation with respect to
$x$ at $x=0$ gives
\[
 \partial_xQ(x,t)=B(t)Q(x,t),
 \qquad B(t)=\partial_xQ(0,t).
\]
The unique solution with $Q(0,t)=1$ is $Q(x,t)=e^{xB(t)}$.  Since
$\deg q_1=1$, the linear coefficient of $B$ is nonzero.
\end{proof}

Let $b(t)=B^{\langle-1\rangle}(t)$ be the compositional inverse.  The formal
differential operator $b(D)$, where $D=\Differential/\Differential x$, is the associated delta
operator.

\begin{proposition}[Lowering operator]
For the sequence in \eqref{new:eq:binomial-type-egf},
\begin{equation}
 b(D)q_n(x)=nq_{n-1}(x).
 \label{new:eq:delta-lowering}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The exponential $e^{xu}$ is an eigenfunction of every formal differential
operator: $b(D)e^{xu}=b(u)e^{xu}$.  Apply this with $u=B(t)$ to the generating
function.  Since $b(B(t))=t$,
\[
 b(D)e^{xB(t)}=t e^{xB(t)}.
\]
Comparing coefficients of $t^n/n!$ proves the result.
\end{proof}

\section{Sheffer sequences}

A sequence $(s_n(x))$ is Sheffer for the pair $(A,B)$ when
$A(0)\ne0$, $B(0)=0$, $B'(0)\ne0$, and
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n\ge0}s_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}
 =A(t)e^{xB(t)}.
 \label{new:eq:sheffer-egf}
\end{equation}
Let $(q_n)$ be the basic binomial-type sequence with EGF $e^{xB(t)}$, and write
$A(t)=\sum a_nt^n/n!$.

\begin{theorem}[Sheffer addition and convolution]
For every $n$,
\begin{align}
 s_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk s_k(x)q_{n-k}(y),
 \label{new:eq:sheffer-addition}\\
 s_n(x)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk a_{n-k}q_k(x).
 \label{new:eq:sheffer-convolution}
\end{align}
Moreover the same delta operator lowers both families:
$b(D)s_n=ns_{n-1}$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Factor the EGF at $x+y$ as
$A(t)e^{xB(t)}e^{yB(t)}$ and use the labelled Cauchy product to obtain
\eqref{new:eq:sheffer-addition}.  Factoring it as $A(t)e^{xB(t)}$ gives
\eqref{new:eq:sheffer-convolution}.  Finally,
$b(D)(A(t)e^{xB(t)})=tA(t)e^{xB(t)}$, and coefficient comparison gives the
lowering identity.
\end{proof}

Appell sequences are the special case $B(t)=t$; Bernoulli and Euler
polynomials below are important examples.  Falling factorials correspond to
$B(t)=\log(1+t)$, and Touchard polynomials correspond to $B(t)=e^t-1$.

\section{Exponential Riordan arrays}

For $g(0)\ne0$ and $f(t)=f_1t+\cdots$ with $f_1\ne0$, define the lower
triangular matrix
\begin{equation}
 R(g,f)_{n,k}=\frac{n!}{k!}[t^n]g(t)f(t)^k.
 \label{new:eq:eriordan-entry}
\end{equation}
If a column vector $(a_k)$ has EGF $A(t)=\sum a_kt^k/k!$, then
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{n\ge0}\left(\sum_{k=0}^nR(g,f)_{n,k}a_k\right)\frac{t^n}{n!}
 =g(t)A(f(t)).
 \label{new:eq:eriordan-action}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Riordan group law]
Exponential Riordan arrays form a group under matrix multiplication, with
\begin{align}
 R(g,f)R(h,\ell)&=R\bigl(g\,h\!\circ f,\,\ell\!\circ f\bigr),
 \label{new:eq:eriordan-product}\\
 R(g,f)^{-1}&=R\left(\frac1{g\circ\bar f},\bar f\right),
 \qquad \bar f=f^{\langle-1\rangle}.
 \label{new:eq:eriordan-inverse}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Apply $R(h,\ell)$ to an arbitrary EGF $A$: it becomes $h(t)A(\ell(t))$.
Applying $R(g,f)$ next gives
\[
 g(t)h(f(t))A(\ell(f(t))),
\]
which is the action of the right side of
\eqref{new:eq:eriordan-product}.  Since two lower triangular matrices agreeing
on every column vector are equal, the product formula follows.  To obtain the
inverse, set $\ell=\bar f$ and choose $h$ so that $g(t)h(f(t))=1$, namely
$h=1/(g\circ\bar f)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[The two Stirling matrices]
The second-kind Stirling matrix is
\[
 \StirlingSecondKind nk=R(1,e^t-1)_{n,k},
\]
while the signed first-kind matrix is
\[
 \SignedStirlingFirstKind{n}{k}=R(1,\log(1+t))_{n,k}.
\]
Because $e^t-1$ and $\log(1+t)$ are compositional inverses,
\eqref{new:eq:eriordan-inverse} proves their matrix inversion in one line.
The same statement was previously proved by changing factorial bases; the two
proofs are complementary views of the same algebra.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Sheffer coefficient arrays]
If $s_n(x)=\sum_k r_{n,k}x^k$, then
\eqref{new:eq:sheffer-egf} gives
$r_{n,k}=R(A,B)_{n,k}$.  Thus the coefficient matrix of every Sheffer sequence
is an exponential Riordan array, and the inverse change of polynomial basis is
computed by \eqref{new:eq:eriordan-inverse}.
\end{example}

\section{Abel polynomials}

For a parameter $a$, define
\[
 A_0(x)=1,\qquad A_n(x)=x(x-an)^{n-1}\quad(n\ge1).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Abel polynomials are of binomial type]
The Abel polynomials satisfy
\begin{equation}
 A_n(x+y)=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk A_k(x)A_{n-k}(y).
 \label{new:eq:abel-binomial}
\end{equation}
Their EGF is $e^{xU(t)}$, where $U$ is determined by
\begin{equation}
 U=t e^{-aU}.
 \label{new:eq:abel-U}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Lagrange--B\"urmann applied to \eqref{new:eq:abel-U} gives, for $n\ge1$,
\[
 [t^n]e^{xU(t)}
 =\frac{x}{n}[u^{n-1}]e^{xu}e^{-anu}
 =\frac{x(x-an)^{n-1}}{n!}.
\]
Thus $e^{xU(t)}=\sum A_n(x)t^n/n!$.  The generating-function
characterization of binomial type now proves \eqref{new:eq:abel-binomial}.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Multivariate Lagrange--Good inversion}
\label{ch:good-inversion}

One-variable Lagrange inversion converts an implicit equation into a coefficient
formula without recursively solving for all earlier coefficients.  For a coupled
system the missing ingredient is a Jacobian determinant.  The following proof is
entirely formal and therefore applies over any characteristic-zero coefficient
ring.

\section{Formal multivariate residues}

For $\boldsymbol z=(z_1,\ldots,z_d)$, put
\[
 \operatorname*{Res}_{\boldsymbol z}
 F(\boldsymbol z)\,\Differential\boldsymbol z
 =[z_1^{-1}\cdots z_d^{-1}]F(\boldsymbol z).
\]
The multivariate substitution law has the same form as the analytic change of
variables formula, provided the substitution has an invertible linear part.
For the special substitution used below it can also be checked directly by
iterated one-variable residue substitutions.

\section{Good's determinant formula}

\begin{theorem}[Multivariate Lagrange--Good inversion]
Let $\boldsymbol w(\boldsymbol t)$ be the unique formal solution of
\begin{equation}
 w_i=t_i\phi_i(\boldsymbol w),\qquad
 \phi_i(\boldsymbol0)\ne0,
 \qquad 1\le i\le d.
 \label{new:eq:good-system}
\end{equation}
For a multi-index $\boldsymbol n=(n_1,\ldots,n_d)\in\NaturalNumbers^d$ and a formal series
$F$, one has
\begin{equation}
 [\boldsymbol t^{\boldsymbol n}]F(\boldsymbol w(\boldsymbol t))
 =[\boldsymbol x^{\boldsymbol n}]
 F(\boldsymbol x)\prod_{i=1}^d\phi_i(\boldsymbol x)^{n_i}
 \det\!\left(
  \delta_{ij}-\frac{x_j}{\phi_i(\boldsymbol x)}
  \frac{\partial\phi_i}{\partial x_j}(\boldsymbol x)
 \right)_{i,j=1}^d.
 \label{new:eq:good-formula}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Represent the desired coefficient as an iterated formal residue:
\[
 [\boldsymbol t^{\boldsymbol n}]F(\boldsymbol w(\boldsymbol t))
 =\operatorname*{Res}_{\boldsymbol t}
 F(\boldsymbol w(\boldsymbol t))
 \prod_{i=1}^dt_i^{-n_i-1}\,\Differential\boldsymbol t.
\]
Make the substitution
$t_i=x_i/\phi_i(\boldsymbol x)$.  Its Jacobian entries are
\[
 \frac{\partial t_i}{\partial x_j}
 =\frac1{\phi_i}
 \left(\delta_{ij}-\frac{x_i}{\phi_i}
       \frac{\partial\phi_i}{\partial x_j}\right).
\]
Therefore
\begin{align*}
 &F(\boldsymbol x)
 \prod_i\left(\frac{x_i}{\phi_i(\boldsymbol x)}\right)^{-n_i-1}
 \det\left(\frac{\partial\boldsymbol t}{\partial\boldsymbol x}\right)
 \\[1mm]
 &\qquad=
 F(\boldsymbol x)\prod_i x_i^{-n_i-1}\phi_i(\boldsymbol x)^{n_i}
 \det\left(
   \delta_{ij}-\frac{x_i}{\phi_i}
   \frac{\partial\phi_i}{\partial x_j}
 \right).
\end{align*}
The determinant here equals the determinant in
\eqref{new:eq:good-formula}.  Indeed, write $X=\DiagonalOperator(x_i)$,
$\Phi=\DiagonalOperator(\phi_i)$, and $J=(\partial_j\phi_i)$.  The first matrix is
$I-X\Phi^{-1}J$, whereas the displayed matrix in the theorem is
$I-\Phi^{-1}JX$; the identity $\det(I-AB)=\det(I-BA)$ shows equality.
Taking the residue in $\boldsymbol x$ is precisely coefficient extraction on
the right side of \eqref{new:eq:good-formula}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Reduction to one variable]
For $d=1$ the determinant is $1-x\phi'(x)/\phi(x)$.  Formal integration by
parts turns Good's formula into
\[
 [t^n]H(w(t))=\frac1n[x^{n-1}]H'(x)\phi(x)^n,
\]
which is Lagrange--B\"urmann.  The determinant is therefore the genuine
multivariate replacement for the one-variable integration-by-parts
simplification.
\end{remark}

\section{A coupled exponential system}

Consider
\begin{equation}
 w_1=t_1e^{w_2},\qquad w_2=t_2e^{w_1}.
 \label{new:eq:coupled-exponential}
\end{equation}
The determinant in Good's formula is
\[
 \det\begin{pmatrix}1&-x_1\\-x_2&1\end{pmatrix}=1-x_1x_2.
\]
For $m\ge1$ and $n\ge0$,
\begin{align*}
 [t_1^mt_2^n]w_1
 &=[x_1^mx_2^n]x_1e^{mx_2+nx_1}(1-x_1x_2).
\end{align*}
When $m\ge1$ and $n\ge1$, direct extraction gives
\begin{equation}
 [t_1^mt_2^n]w_1
 =\frac{n^{m-1}m^{n-1}}{(m-1)!\,n!}.
 \label{new:eq:coupled-coefficient}
\end{equation}
Indeed, the first determinant term contributes
$n^{m-1}m^n/((m-1)!n!)$ and the second contributes $(m-1)/m$ times that
quantity.  Their difference is \eqref{new:eq:coupled-coefficient}.  This
example illustrates the role of the determinant: omitting it produces a
systematic overcount.

\section{Analytic obstruction and the characteristic determinant}

Suppose now that the $\phi_i$ are analytic near the origin.  The implicit
function theorem continues the solution as long as the Jacobian of the map
\[
 \boldsymbol x\longmapsto
 \left(\frac{x_1}{\phi_1(\boldsymbol x)},\ldots,
       \frac{x_d}{\phi_d(\boldsymbol x)}\right)
\]
remains nonsingular and no $\phi_i$ becomes singular.  Its determinant differs
from the determinant in \eqref{new:eq:good-formula} only by the nonzero factor
$\prod_i\phi_i^{-1}$.  Thus the same determinant that corrects formal
coefficient extraction also identifies the characteristic variety on which
analytic inversion can break down.

\chapter{Classical reversion: Kepler's equation}
\label{ch:kepler}

Kepler's equation is an instructive bridge between local power-series reversion
and global Fourier analysis.  For eccentricity $|e|<1$, it is
\begin{equation}
 M=E-e\sin E.
 \label{new:eq:kepler}
\end{equation}
Since $1-e\cos E>0$ on the real axis, it has a unique real solution $E=E(M)$,
and $E(M+2\pi)=E(M)+2\pi$.

\section{Lagrange's expansion in the eccentricity}

Put $u=E-M$.  Then
\[
 u=e\sin(M+u).
\]
Treating $M$ as a parameter, Lagrange inversion gives the convergent expansion
near $e=0$
\begin{equation}
 E=M+\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{e^r}{r!}
 \frac{\Differential^{r-1}}{\Differential M^{r-1}}\sin^r M.
 \label{new:eq:kepler-lagrange}
\end{equation}
The first terms are
\[
 E=M+e\sin M+\frac{e^2}{2}\sin2M
 +\frac{e^3}{8}(3\sin3M-\sin M)+O(e^4).
\]
The formula is a direct instance of reversion with an offset and does not
require any special-function theory.

\section{The Fourier--Bessel expansion}

Let $J_n$ denote the Bessel function of the first kind, characterized for
integer $n$ by
\begin{equation}
 J_n(z)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
 \cos(n\theta-z\sin\theta)\,\Differential\theta.
 \label{new:eq:bessel-integral}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Fourier solution of Kepler's equation]
For $|e|<1$,
\begin{equation}
 E(M)=M+2\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{J_n(ne)}{n}\sin(nM).
 \label{new:eq:kepler-bessel}
\end{equation}
The series converges absolutely and locally uniformly for real $M$ and
$e$ in compact subsets of $(-1,1)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The periodic function $u(M)=E(M)-M$ is odd.  Write
$u(M)=\sum_{n\ge1}b_n\sin(nM)$.  Its sine coefficient is
\[
 b_n=\frac1\pi\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}u(M)\sin(nM)\,\Differential M.
\]
Because $u(\pm\pi)=0$, integration by parts gives
\[
 b_n=\frac1{n\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}u'(M)\cos(nM)\,\Differential M.
\]
Under the change of variable $M=E-e\sin E$,
\[
 u'(M)\,\Differential M=\Differential E-\Differential M=e\cos E\,\Differential E.
\]
Hence, with $\Theta(E)=n(E-e\sin E)$,
\begin{equation}
 b_n=\frac{e}{n\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}
 \cos E\cos\Theta(E)\,\Differential E.
 \label{new:eq:kepler-bn-intermediate}
\end{equation}
The integral of the derivative of $\sin\Theta(E)$ over a full period is zero:
\[
 0=n\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}(1-e\cos E)\cos\Theta(E)\,\Differential E.
\]
Therefore
\[
 e\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\cos E\cos\Theta(E)\,\Differential E
 =\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\cos\Theta(E)\,\Differential E
 =2\pi J_n(ne),
\]
where the last equality is \eqref{new:eq:bessel-integral}.  Substitution into
\eqref{new:eq:kepler-bn-intermediate} yields $b_n=2J_n(ne)/n$.

For $|e|<1$, the derivative $1-e\cos E$ has no real zero.  Compactness and the
analytic implicit-function theorem continue $u$ to a complex strip whose width
is positive uniformly when $e$ ranges over a compact subset of $(-1,1)$.
Fourier coefficients of a function analytic in a strip decay exponentially,
which proves absolute and locally uniform convergence.
\end{proof}

The Lagrange series \eqref{new:eq:kepler-lagrange} organizes the answer by
powers of $e$; the Bessel series \eqref{new:eq:kepler-bessel} organizes it by
Fourier modes in $M$.  Expanding $J_n(ne)$ in powers of $e$ and rearranging
recovers the Lagrange series term by term.

\chapter{Catalan refinements and associahedral faces}
\label{ch:associahedron-refinements}

The supplied reference material emphasizes three related objects: Catalan
numbers, triangulations, and associahedra.  The preceding chapters counted
vertices.  Here Lagrange inversion counts every face and simultaneously derives
the Narayana refinement by peaks of a Dyck path.

\section{Narayana numbers from first-return decomposition}

Let $C(z,u)$ count Dyck paths, where $z$ marks semilength and $u$ marks peaks.
A nonempty Dyck path has a unique first-return decomposition $UADB$.  If $A$ is
empty, the initial $UD$ is a peak; otherwise it is not.  Therefore
\begin{equation}
 C(z,u)=1+zC(z,u)\bigl(C(z,u)+u-1\bigr).
 \label{new:eq:dyck-bivariate}
\end{equation}
Put $Y=C-1$.  Then $Y=z(1+Y)(u+Y)$.  Lagrange inversion gives
\begin{align*}
 [z^nu^k]Y
 &=\frac1n[y^{n-1}u^k](1+y)^n(u+y)^n\\
 &=\frac1n\binom nk\binom n{k-1}.
\end{align*}
Thus the number of Dyck paths of semilength $n$ with $k$ peaks is the Narayana
number
\begin{equation}
 N(n,k)=\frac1n\binom nk\binom n{k-1},
 \qquad 1\le k\le n.
 \label{new:eq:narayana}
\end{equation}
Summing over $k$ and using Vandermonde's identity gives
\[
 \sum_{k=1}^nN(n,k)
 =\frac1n\binom{2n}{n-1}
 =\frac1{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}=\CatalanNumber n.
\]
The Narayana row is also the $h$-vector of the type-$A$ associahedron; the Dyck
path statistic supplies a combinatorial model for those entries.

\section{The Kirkman--Cayley dissection formula}

A face of codimension $r$ in the associahedron associated with an $N$-gon is a
set of $r$ pairwise noncrossing diagonals.  Such a set dissects the polygon into
$r+1$ cells.

\begin{theorem}[Polygon dissections]
The number of ways to dissect a convex $N$-gon by $r$ noncrossing diagonals is
\begin{equation}
 D(N,r)=\frac1{r+1}\binom{N+r-1}{r}\binom{N-3}{r},
 \qquad 0\le r\le N-3.
 \label{new:eq:kirkman-cayley}
\end{equation}
Consequently the number of $(n-k)$-dimensional faces of
$\AssociahedronByDimension{n-1}$ is
\begin{equation}
 \frac1k\binom{n+k}{k-1}\binom{n-1}{k-1},
 \qquad 1\le k\le n.
 \label{new:eq:associa-face}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use the planar dual of a dissection, rooted at a boundary edge.  Boundary edges
other than the root become leaves, and cells become internal vertices of
outdegree at least two.  If $z$ marks leaves and $u$ marks internal vertices,
the corresponding Schr\"oder trees satisfy
\begin{equation}
 T=z+u\frac{T^2}{1-T}.
 \label{new:eq:schroeder-tree}
\end{equation}
Indeed, an internal root has an ordered sequence of at least two subtrees.
Rearrange this as
\[
 T=z\frac{1-T}{1-(1+u)T}.
\]
Lagrange inversion yields, for $m\ge1$ and $q\ge1$,
\begin{align*}
 [z^mu^q]T
 &=\frac1m[t^{m-1}u^q]
   \left(\frac{1-t}{1-(1+u)t}\right)^m\\
 &=\frac1m[t^{m-1}u^q]
   \left(1-\frac{ut}{1-t}\right)^{-m}\\
 &=\frac1m\binom{m+q-1}{q}\binom{m-2}{q-1}.
\end{align*}
An $N$-gon contributes $m=N-1$ nonroot boundary edges, and a dissection by
$r$ diagonals has $q=r+1$ cells.  Substitution gives
\[
 \frac1{N-1}\binom{N+r-1}{r+1}\binom{N-3}{r}
 =\frac1{r+1}\binom{N+r-1}{r}\binom{N-3}{r},
\]
which is \eqref{new:eq:kirkman-cayley}.  Finally $\AssociahedronByDimension{n-1}$ corresponds to an
$(n+2)$-gon and codimension $k-1$, so $N=n+2$ and $r=k-1$ give
\eqref{new:eq:associa-face}.
\end{proof}

For $\AssociahedronByDimension3$ the formula gives $1,9,21,14$ faces in dimensions $3,2,1,0$,
respectively, matching the polyhedral data in the supplied associahedron
reference.


\part{Bernoulli, Euler, Genocchi, and Summation Calculus}

\chapter{Bernoulli polynomials and finite power sums}
\label{ch:bernoulli-polynomials}

Bernoulli numbers appeared earlier through alternating Eulerian sums and
Stirling transforms.  The polynomial family places those identities in an
Appell sequence and makes finite summation an operator inverse to forward
difference.

\section{Definition and elementary identities}

Define the Bernoulli polynomials $\beta_n(x)$ by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{t e^{xt}}{e^t-1}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}\beta_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}.
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-polynomial-egf}
\end{equation}
Thus $\beta_n=\beta_n(0)$ are the Bernoulli numbers in the convention
$\beta_1=-1/2$.

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli polynomial calculus]
For $n\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 \beta_n(x)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk\beta_k x^{n-k},
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-explicit}\\
 \beta_n'(x)&=n\beta_{n-1}(x),
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-appell}\\
 \beta_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk\beta_k(x)y^{n-k},
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-addition}\\
 \beta_n(x+1)-\beta_n(x)&=n x^{n-1}\qquad(n\ge1),
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-difference}\\
 \beta_n(1-x)&=(-1)^n\beta_n(x),
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-reflection}\\
 \beta_n(x)&=m^{n-1}\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}
 \beta_n\!\left(\frac{x+r}{m}\right)\qquad(m\ge1).
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-multiplication}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Factor \eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-polynomial-egf} as
$e^{xt}\sum\beta_kt^k/k!$ and compare coefficients to obtain
\eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-explicit}.  Differentiation with respect to $x$ proves
\eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-appell}; multiplying by $e^{yt}$ proves the addition
law.

For the difference law, subtract the generating functions at $x+1$ and $x$:
\[
 \frac{te^{(x+1)t}-te^{xt}}{e^t-1}=te^{xt}.
\]
Coefficient extraction gives \eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-difference}.  Replacing
$t$ by $-t$ in the EGF and simplifying gives
\[
 \frac{-te^{-(1-x)t}}{e^{-t}-1}
 =\frac{te^{xt}}{e^t-1},
\]
which proves reflection.

For multiplication, compute the EGF of the right side:
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{n\ge0}m^{n-1}\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}
 \beta_n\!\left(\frac{x+r}{m}\right)\frac{t^n}{n!}
 &=\frac1m\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}
   \frac{mt\,e^{(x+r)t}}{e^{mt}-1}\\
 &=\frac{te^{xt}}{e^{mt}-1}\sum_{r=0}^{m-1}e^{rt}\\
 &=\frac{te^{xt}}{e^t-1}.
\end{align*}
This proves \eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-multiplication}.
\end{proof}

\section{Faulhaber's formula}

The difference identity telescopes immediately.

\begin{corollary}[Finite power sums]
For $p\ge0$, $N\ge0$, and arbitrary $x$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{N-1}(x+j)^p
 =\frac{\beta_{p+1}(x+N)-\beta_{p+1}(x)}{p+1}.
 \label{new:eq:faulhaber-bernoulli}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=1}^{N}j^p
 =\frac{\beta_{p+1}(N+1)-\beta_{p+1}}{p+1}.
 \label{new:eq:faulhaber-standard}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply \eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-difference} with $n=p+1$ and argument $x+j$,
then sum from $j=0$ to $N-1$.  All intermediate Bernoulli-polynomial values
cancel.
\end{proof}

This proof explains the role of Bernoulli polynomials: they are the canonical
polynomial antiderivatives for the forward-difference operator.

\section{Stirling and finite-difference representations}

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli polynomials from finite differences]
For every $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \beta_n(x)=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}\Delta^k x^n,
 \qquad
 \Delta^k x^n=\sum_{j=0}^k(-1)^{k-j}\binom kj(x+j)^n.
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-finite-difference}
\end{equation}
At $x=0$ this becomes the Bernoulli--Stirling formula
\begin{equation}
 \beta_n=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k\frac{k!}{k+1}\StirlingSecondKind nk.
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-stirling}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
In the ring of formal series,
\begin{align*}
 \frac{te^{xt}}{e^t-1}
 &=e^{xt}\int_0^1\frac{\Differential u}{1+u(e^t-1)}\\
 &=\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(-1)^k}{k+1}
   e^{xt}(e^t-1)^k.
\end{align*}
The coefficient of $t^n/n!$ in $e^{xt}(e^t-1)^k$ is $\Delta^kx^n$,
which proves \eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-finite-difference}.  At $x=0$ this
coefficient is $k!\StirlingSecondKind nk$, giving
\eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-stirling}.
\end{proof}

\section{Fourier series and even zeta values}

\begin{theorem}[Fourier expansion]
For $n\ge2$ and $0\le x\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 \beta_n(x)=-n!\sum_{k\in\IntegerNumbers\setminus\{0\}}
 \frac{e^{2\pi ikx}}{(2\pi ik)^n}.
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-fourier-complex}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for $m\ge1$,
\begin{align}
 \beta_{2m}(x)
 &=(-1)^{m+1}\frac{2(2m)!}{(2\pi)^{2m}}
   \sum_{k\ge1}\frac{\cos(2\pi kx)}{k^{2m}},
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-fourier-even}\\
 \beta_{2m+1}(x)
 &=(-1)^{m+1}\frac{2(2m+1)!}{(2\pi)^{2m+1}}
   \sum_{k\ge1}\frac{\sin(2\pi kx)}{k^{2m+1}}.
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-fourier-odd}
\end{align}
In particular,
\begin{equation}
 \RiemannZeta(2m)=(-1)^{m+1}
 \frac{(2\pi)^{2m}\beta_{2m}}{2(2m)!}.
 \label{new:eq:zeta-even-bernoulli}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $c_k^{(n)}=\int_0^1\beta_n(x)e^{-2\pi ikx}\,\Differential x$.  For $k\ne0$ and
$n\ge2$, the endpoint values agree by
\eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-difference}, so integration by parts and the Appell
identity give
\[
 c_k^{(n)}=\frac{n}{2\pi ik}c_k^{(n-1)}.
\]
At the last step,
\[
 c_k^{(1)}=\int_0^1\left(x-\frac12\right)e^{-2\pi ikx}\,\Differential x
 =-\frac1{2\pi ik}.
\]
Thus $c_k^{(n)}=-n!/(2\pi ik)^n$.  The mean coefficient is zero for $n\ge1$
because $\int_0^1\beta_n(x)\Differential x=0$, a consequence of the Appell identity and
$\beta_{n+1}(1)=\beta_{n+1}(0)$.  This proves the complex Fourier series.
Pairing $k$ with $-k$ gives the sine and cosine forms.  Setting $x=0$ in the
even formula gives \eqref{new:eq:zeta-even-bernoulli}.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Euler polynomials and Genocchi numbers}
\label{ch:euler-genocchi}

Define the Euler polynomials by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{2e^{xt}}{e^t+1}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}E_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}.
 \label{new:eq:euler-polynomial-egf}
\end{equation}
They are an Appell sequence, but forward difference is replaced by a symmetric
average.

\begin{theorem}[Euler polynomial calculus]
For $n\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 E_n'(x)&=nE_{n-1}(x),
 \label{new:eq:euler-appell}\\
 E_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nkE_k(x)y^{n-k},
 \label{new:eq:euler-addition}\\
 E_n(x+1)+E_n(x)&=2x^n,
 \label{new:eq:euler-difference}\\
 E_n(1-x)&=(-1)^nE_n(x),
 \label{new:eq:euler-reflection}\\
 E_n(x)&=\frac{2}{n+1}
 \left(\beta_{n+1}(x)-2^{n+1}\beta_{n+1}\!\left(\frac x2\right)\right).
 \label{new:eq:euler-bernoulli}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiation and multiplication by $e^{yt}$ prove the first two identities.
Adding the EGFs at $x+1$ and $x$ gives $2e^{xt}$, proving
\eqref{new:eq:euler-difference}; replacing $t$ by $-t$ proves reflection.
Finally,
\[
 \frac{2}{e^t+1}=\frac{2}{e^t-1}-\frac{4}{e^{2t}-1}.
\]
Multiply by $e^{xt}$, express both terms through Bernoulli-polynomial EGFs, and
compare the coefficient of $t^n/n!$.  The shift by one index caused by the
factor $1/t$ gives exactly \eqref{new:eq:euler-bernoulli}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Alternating power sums]
For $m\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(-1)^j(x+j)^n
 =\frac{E_n(x)-(-1)^mE_n(x+m)}{2}.
 \label{new:eq:alternating-power-sum}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use $2(x+j)^n=E_n(x+j+1)+E_n(x+j)$, multiply by $(-1)^j$, and sum.  The
interior terms cancel in pairs.
\end{proof}

The centered values
\[
 \mathcal E_n=2^nE_n\!\left(\frac12\right)
\]
are the classical Euler numbers in the hyperbolic-secant convention, because
\[
 \operatorname{sech}t=\sum_{n\ge0}\mathcal E_n\frac{t^n}{n!}.
\]
Reflection about $1/2$ makes every odd $\mathcal E_n$ vanish.

\section{Genocchi numbers}

Define $\GenocchiNumber n$ by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{2t}{e^t+1}=\sum_{n\ge0}\GenocchiNumber n\frac{t^n}{n!}.
 \label{new:eq:genocchi-egf}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Bernoulli and Euler representations]
For all $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \GenocchiNumber n=2(1-2^n)\beta_n=nE_{n-1}(0)\quad(n\ge1),
 \label{new:eq:genocchi-relations}
\end{equation}
with $\GenocchiNumber0=0$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Use
\[
 \frac{2t}{e^t+1}=\frac{2t}{e^t-1}-\frac{4t}{e^{2t}-1}.
\]
The first term contributes $2\beta_n$ and the second contributes
$2^{n+1}\beta_n$, proving the first equality.  On the other hand,
$t\cdot 2/(e^t+1)$ is $t$ times the Euler-polynomial EGF at $x=0$; shifting
indices gives $\GenocchiNumber n=nE_{n-1}(0)$.
\end{proof}

Since $\beta_n=0$ for odd $n>1$, the same is true of $\GenocchiNumber n$.  The first values
are
\[
 \GenocchiNumber0=0,\quad \GenocchiNumber1=1,\quad \GenocchiNumber2=-1,\quad \GenocchiNumber4=1,\quad
 \GenocchiNumber6=-3,\quad \GenocchiNumber8=17.
\]

\section{Modified Bernoulli coefficients}

A small sequence in the supplied MathWorld material is generated by
\begin{equation}
 \frac12\log\left(\frac{e^{x/2}-e^{-x/2}}{x/2}\right)
 =\frac12\log2+\sum_{r\ge1}\mathfrak b_{2r}x^{2r}.
 \label{new:eq:modified-bernoulli-egf}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}
The odd coefficients vanish, and
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak b_{2r}=\frac{\beta_{2r}}{4r(2r)!}.
 \label{new:eq:modified-bernoulli}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for even $n\ge2$,
$\mathfrak b_n=\beta_n/(2n\,n!)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the left side of \eqref{new:eq:modified-bernoulli-egf}:
\[
 \frac14\coth\frac x2-\frac1{2x}.
\]
The Bernoulli generating function implies
\[
 \coth\frac x2=\frac2x+
 \sum_{r\ge1}\frac{2\beta_{2r}}{(2r)!}x^{2r-1}.
\]
After cancellation of $1/(2x)$, integrate term by term and use the limiting
constant $\frac12\log2$.
\end{proof}

\chapter{Bernoulli polynomials of the second kind and complementary Bell numbers}
\label{ch:second-bernoulli-complementary-bell}

The phrase ``Bernoulli numbers of the second kind'' refers to a different
Sheffer sequence, also called the Cauchy numbers of the first kind.  Their
appearance in the reference folder is natural here because their coefficients
are signed first-kind Stirling numbers, whereas complementary Bell numbers are
an alternating second-kind Stirling transform.

\section{The second-kind Bernoulli--Cauchy sequence}

Define $c_n(x)$ by
\begin{equation}
 \frac{t(1+t)^x}{\log(1+t)}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}c_n(x)\frac{t^n}{n!}.
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-second-egf}
\end{equation}
This is a Sheffer sequence with prefactor $t/\log(1+t)$ and basic sequence
$\FallingFactorial{x}{n}$.

\begin{theorem}[Integral, derivative, and Stirling formulas]
For $n\ge0$,
\begin{align}
 c_n(x)&=\int_0^1\FallingFactorial{x+u}{n}\,\Differential u,
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-second-integral}\\
 c_n'(x)&=n\FallingFactorial{x}{n-1},
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-second-derivative}\\
 c_n(x+1)-c_n(x)&=nc_{n-1}(x),
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-second-difference}\\
 c_n(x+y)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk c_k(x)\FallingFactorial y{n-k},
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-second-addition}\\
 c_n(0)&=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{\SignedStirlingFirstKind{n}{k}}{k+1},
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-second-stirling}\\
 c_n(x)&=c_n(0)+\sum_{k=1}^n\frac nk \SignedStirlingFirstKind{n-1}{k-1}x^k.
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-second-explicit}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since
\[
 \frac{t}{\log(1+t)}=\int_0^1(1+t)^u\,\Differential u,
\]
multiplication by $(1+t)^x$ and coefficient extraction prove the integral
formula.  Differentiating the EGF with respect to $x$ gives
$t(1+t)^x$, whose coefficient is
$n\FallingFactorial{x}{n-1}$; this proves the derivative formula.  Replacing $x$ by
$x+1$ and subtracting multiplies the EGF by $t$, giving the difference formula.
The Sheffer addition law gives \eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-second-addition}.

At $x=0$, expand $\FallingFactorial u n=\sum_k \SignedStirlingFirstKind{n}{k}u^k$ in the integral to obtain
\eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-second-stirling}.  Finally integrate
$c_n'(x)=n\sum_j \SignedStirlingFirstKind{n-1}{j}x^j$ from $0$ to $x$, and put $k=j+1$.
\end{proof}

The reflection formula is centered at $n/2-1$.

\begin{proposition}[Reflection]
\begin{equation}
 c_n\!\left(\frac n2-1-x\right)
 =(-1)^n c_n\!\left(\frac n2-1+x\right).
 \label{new:eq:bernoulli-second-reflection}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The falling factorial satisfies
$\FallingFactorial y n=(-1)^n\FallingFactorial{n-1-y}{n}$.  Apply this in the integral
representation and change $u$ to $1-u$ to get
$c_n(x)=(-1)^nc_n(n-2-x)$.  Recenter by writing
$x=n/2-1+v$.
\end{proof}

The first polynomials are
\begin{align*}
 c_0(x)&=1,\\
 c_1(x)&=x+\frac12,\\
 c_2(x)&=x^2-\frac16,\\
 c_3(x)&=x^3-\frac32x^2+\frac14,\\
 c_4(x)&=x^4-4x^3+4x^2-\frac{19}{30}.
\end{align*}

\section{Complementary Bell numbers}

Define
\begin{equation}
 \ComplementaryBellNumber n=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k\StirlingSecondKind nk.
 \label{new:eq:complementary-bell-def}
\end{equation}
Thus $\ComplementaryBellNumber n$ is the difference between the numbers of set partitions with an even
and an odd number of blocks, with the sign convention in
\eqref{new:eq:complementary-bell-def}.  Equivalently, $\ComplementaryBellNumber n=\TouchardPolynomial n(-1)$ for the
Touchard polynomial $\TouchardPolynomial n(x)$.

\begin{theorem}[Complementary Bell calculus]
The numbers $\ComplementaryBellNumber n$ satisfy
\begin{align}
 \sum_{n\ge0}\ComplementaryBellNumber n\frac{t^n}{n!}&=\exp(1-e^t),
 \label{new:eq:complementary-bell-egf}\\
 \ComplementaryBellNumber{n+1}&=-\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk\ComplementaryBellNumber k,
 \label{new:eq:complementary-bell-recurrence}\\
 \ComplementaryBellNumber n&=e\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{(-1)^m m^n}{m!}.
 \label{new:eq:complementary-bell-dobinski}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Insert $x=-1$ into the Touchard EGF
$\sum \TouchardPolynomial n(x)t^n/n!=\exp(x(e^t-1))$ to obtain
\eqref{new:eq:complementary-bell-egf}.  Differentiation gives
$F'(t)=-e^tF(t)$; the labelled Cauchy product yields the recurrence.  Finally,
expand
\[
 e^{1-e^t}=e\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{(-1)^m e^{mt}}{m!}
\]
and compare coefficients.  Absolute convergence justifies the interchange for
every complex $t$.
\end{proof}

The initial values are
\[
 1,-1,0,1,1,-2,-9,-9,50,267,413,\ldots.
\]
The formula is the exact analogue of Dobi\'nski's representation of ordinary
Bell numbers, with the Poisson weight analytically continued from $+1$ to
$-1$.

\chapter{Euler--Maclaurin summation with a controlled remainder}
\label{ch:euler-maclaurin}

The Bernoulli-polynomial difference law is the algebraic core of the
Euler--Maclaurin formula.  The analytic formula repeatedly transfers
derivatives from a function to periodic Bernoulli polynomials, converting a
sum into an integral plus endpoint corrections.

\section{Periodic Bernoulli functions}

For $r\ge1$, let
\[
 \widetilde\beta_r(x)=\beta_r(\{x\}),
\]
where $\{x\}$ is the fractional part.  For $r\ge2$ this is continuous at
integers because $\beta_r(1)=\beta_r(0)$; for $r=1$ it is the sawtooth function
$x-\Floor{x}-1/2$ away from integers.  On every open unit interval,
\[
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential x}\widetilde\beta_r(x)
 =r\widetilde\beta_{r-1}(x).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Euler--Maclaurin formula]
Let $a<b$ be integers, let $p\ge1$, and let $f\in C^{2p}([a,b])$.  Then
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k=a}^{b}f(k)
 &=\int_a^bf(x)\,\Differential x+\frac{f(a)+f(b)}2
 +\sum_{r=1}^{p}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{(2r)!}
  \bigl(f^{(2r-1)}(b)-f^{(2r-1)}(a)\bigr)
 +R_p,
 \label{new:eq:euler-maclaurin}\\
 R_p&=-\frac1{(2p)!}\int_a^b
 \widetilde\beta_{2p}(x)f^{(2p)}(x)\,\Differential x.
 \label{new:eq:euler-maclaurin-remainder}
\end{align}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 |R_p|\le
 \frac{2\RiemannZeta(2p)}{(2\pi)^{2p}}
 \int_a^b|f^{(2p)}(x)|\,\Differential x.
 \label{new:eq:euler-maclaurin-bound}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
On one interval $[k,k+1]$, integration by parts gives
\[
 \int_k^{k+1}f(x)\,\Differential x
 =\frac{f(k)+f(k+1)}2
 -\int_k^{k+1}\left(x-k-\frac12\right)f'(x)\,\Differential x.
\]
Sum from $k=a$ to $b-1$ and rearrange:
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{k=a}^bf(k)
 =\int_a^bf(x)\,\Differential x+\frac{f(a)+f(b)}2
 +\int_a^b\widetilde\beta_1(x)f'(x)\,\Differential x.
 \label{new:eq:em-start}
\end{equation}
On each unit interval, integrate the last integral by parts using
$\widetilde\beta_2/2!$ as an antiderivative.  The boundary contributions at
interior integers cancel because $\widetilde\beta_2$ is continuous and has the
common endpoint value $\beta_2$.  This gives the first correction and a
remainder involving $\widetilde\beta_2f''$ with a minus sign.

Continue.  The boundary terms involving odd Bernoulli polynomials beyond
$\beta_1$ vanish because $\beta_{2r+1}(0)=\beta_{2r+1}(1)=0$ for $r\ge1$.
Every second integration therefore contributes
$\beta_{2r}(f^{(2r-1)}(b)-f^{(2r-1)}(a))/(2r)!$.  Stopping after the
$2p$th derivative gives \eqref{new:eq:euler-maclaurin} and
\eqref{new:eq:euler-maclaurin-remainder}.

The Fourier series \eqref{new:eq:bernoulli-fourier-even} implies
\[
 |\widetilde\beta_{2p}(x)|
 \le\frac{2(2p)!}{(2\pi)^{2p}}\RiemannZeta(2p).
\]
Insert this in the remainder integral to obtain
\eqref{new:eq:euler-maclaurin-bound}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
If $f$ is a polynomial of degree at most $2p-1$, the remainder vanishes.
Euler--Maclaurin then reduces to an exact polynomial identity and recovers
Faulhaber's formula.  For general analytic $f$, the series of correction terms
is usually asymptotic rather than convergent; the integral remainder specifies
exactly what truncation means.
\end{remark}

\section{Harmonic numbers}

Applying Euler--Maclaurin to $f(x)=1/x$ and passing to an infinite endpoint
gives, for fixed $p\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 H_n=\log n+\gamma+\frac1{2n}
 -\sum_{r=1}^{p-1}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{2r\,n^{2r}}
 +O_p(n^{-2p}).
 \label{new:eq:harmonic-em}
\end{equation}
Here $\gamma=\lim_{N\to\infty}(H_N-\log N)$.  To see the coefficients,
note that
$f^{(2r-1)}(x)=-(2r-1)!x^{-2r}$.  The remainder estimate follows directly
from \eqref{new:eq:euler-maclaurin-bound} on the tail interval.

\section{Stirling's expansion}

Apply Euler--Maclaurin to $f(x)=\log x$.  The derivative
$f^{(m)}(x)=(-1)^{m-1}(m-1)!x^{-m}$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \log n!
 =\left(n+\frac12\right)\log n-n+C
 +\sum_{r=1}^{p-1}
  \frac{\beta_{2r}}{2r(2r-1)n^{2r-1}}
 +O_p(n^{-(2p-1)}).
 \label{new:eq:stirling-em-constant}
\end{equation}
The constant is independent of $p$.  Wallis' product determines it.
Indeed, subtracting the corresponding expansions for $(2n)!$, $n!$, and the
central binomial coefficient, and comparing with
\[
 \binom{2n}{n}\sim\frac{4^n}{\sqrt{\pi n}},
\]
which follows from Wallis' inequalities, gives $C=\frac12\log(2\pi)$.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 \log n!
 \sim\left(n+\frac12\right)\log n-n+\frac12\log(2\pi)
 +\sum_{r\ge1}\frac{\beta_{2r}}{2r(2r-1)n^{2r-1}}.
 \label{new:eq:stirling-log}
\end{equation}
Exponentiation begins with
\begin{equation}
 n!=\sqrt{2\pi n}\left(\frac ne\right)^n
 \left(1+\frac1{12n}+\frac1{288n^2}
       -\frac{139}{51840n^3}+O(n^{-4})\right).
 \label{new:eq:stirling-factorial}
\end{equation}
The coefficients in the exponential form are complete Bell polynomials in the
Bernoulli correction terms, providing another direct connection with the
coefficient calculus of this monograph.


\part{Gamma Products, Barnes Functions, and Darboux Asymptotics}

\chapter{Pochhammer symbols and polygamma derivative calculus}
\label{ch:pochhammer-polygamma}

The rising factorial is simultaneously a polynomial basis, a gamma quotient,
and the coefficient sequence of the generalized binomial theorem.  Its
parameter derivatives are naturally expressed by complete Bell polynomials in
finite polygamma differences.

\section{Equivalent definitions}

For $n\in\NaturalNumbers$, the Pochhammer symbol is
\[
 (z)_n=\RisingFactorial z n=z(z+1)\cdots(z+n-1),\qquad (z)_0=1.
\]
Whenever neither side encounters an uncancelled pole,
\begin{equation}
 (z)_n=\frac{\Gamma(z+n)}{\Gamma(z)}.
 \label{new:eq:pochhammer-gamma}
\end{equation}
This follows by iterating $\Gamma(z+1)=z\Gamma(z)$; since both sides are
polynomials after cancellation, the identity extends to all $z$.

\begin{theorem}[Binomial and Stirling expansions]
For formal $t$,
\begin{align}
 (1-t)^{-z}&=\sum_{n\ge0}(z)_n\frac{t^n}{n!},
 \label{new:eq:pochhammer-binomial}\\
 (z)_n&=\sum_{k=0}^n\UnsignedStirlingFirstKind nk z^k,
 \label{new:eq:pochhammer-stirling}\\
 (-z)_n&=(-1)^n\FallingFactorial z n.
 \label{new:eq:pochhammer-falling}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The series on the right of \eqref{new:eq:pochhammer-binomial} equals $1$ at
$t=0$.  Its derivative is
\[
 \sum_{n\ge0}(z)_{n+1}\frac{t^n}{n!}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}(z+n)(z)_n\frac{t^n}{n!}
 =zF(t)+tF'(t).
\]
Thus $(1-t)F'(t)=zF(t)$, whose unique formal solution with $F(0)=1$ is
$(1-t)^{-z}$.  The Stirling expansion is the defining change of basis between
powers and rising factorials, already proved combinatorially by permutation
cycles.  The last identity follows by reversing the signs of all $n$ factors.
\end{proof}

A useful forward-difference identity is
\begin{equation}
 (z+1)_n-(z)_n=n(z+1)_{n-1}.
 \label{new:eq:pochhammer-difference}
\end{equation}
It follows by factoring $(z+1)_{n-1}$ from both terms.

\section{Polygamma differences}

Let
\[
 \psi(z)=\frac{\Gamma'(z)}{\Gamma(z)},
 \qquad \psi^{(r)}(z)=\frac{\Differential^r}{\Differential z^r}\psi(z).
\]
Logarithmic differentiation of the gamma recurrence gives
\begin{equation}
 \psi^{(r)}(z+1)-\psi^{(r)}(z)
 =(-1)^r\frac{r!}{z^{r+1}}.
 \label{new:eq:polygamma-step}
\end{equation}
Iteration yields the finite difference
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_r(z,n):=\psi^{(r)}(z+n)-\psi^{(r)}(z)
 =(-1)^rr!\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac1{(z+j)^{r+1}}.
 \label{new:eq:polygamma-finite}
\end{equation}
Although the terms on the right may have poles at nonpositive integers, the
final Pochhammer derivative formulas below extend polynomially after
cancellation.

\begin{theorem}[Parameter derivatives of a Pochhammer symbol]
For $m,n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\Differential^m}{\Differential z^m}(z)_n
 =(z)_n\,
 \ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial m\bigl(\Delta_0(z,n),\Delta_1(z,n),\ldots,
                    \Delta_{m-1}(z,n)\bigr).
 \label{new:eq:pochhammer-derivative}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $(z)_n=e^{L(z)}$ with
$L(z)=\log\Gamma(z+n)-\log\Gamma(z)$.  Then
$L^{(r+1)}(z)=\Delta_r(z,n)$.  The exponential specialization of Fa\`a di
Bruno states
\[
 \frac{\Differential^m}{\Differential z^m}e^{L(z)}
 =e^{L(z)}\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial m(L',L'',\ldots,L^{(m)}),
\]
which gives the formula.
\end{proof}

For example,
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential z}(z)_n
 &=(z)_n\Delta_0,\\
 \frac{\Differential^2}{\Differential z^2}(z)_n
 &=(z)_n(\Delta_0^2+\Delta_1),\\
 \frac{\Differential^3}{\Differential z^3}(z)_n
 &=(z)_n(\Delta_0^3+3\Delta_0\Delta_1+\Delta_2).
\end{align*}
At $z=1$, let $H_n^{(r)}=\sum_{j=1}^n j^{-r}$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \left.\frac{\Differential^m}{\Differential z^m}(z)_n\right|_{z=1}
 =n!\ExponentialCompleteBellPolynomial m\bigl(H_n,-1!H_n^{(2)},2!H_n^{(3)},\ldots,
                  (-1)^{m-1}(m-1)!H_n^{(m)}\bigr).
 \label{new:eq:pochhammer-harmonic}
\end{equation}
The first three values are
\begin{align*}
 n!H_n,\qquad
 n!\bigl(H_n^2-H_n^{(2)}\bigr),\qquad
 n!\bigl(H_n^3-3H_nH_n^{(2)}+2H_n^{(3)}\bigr).
\end{align*}
These are the same harmonic combinations that describe near-diagonal
first-kind Stirling numbers, because both arise from elementary symmetric
functions in reciprocal linear factors.

\chapter{The Barnes \texorpdfstring{$\BarnesGFunction$}{G}-function}
\label{ch:barnes-g}

The gamma function interpolates factorials; the Barnes $\BarnesGFunction$-function interpolates
products of factorials.  It is therefore the natural special function for
triangular products that arise after summing logarithms or integrating
$\log\Gamma$.

\section{Canonical product and functional equation}

Define
\begin{equation}
 \begin{split}
 \BarnesGFunction(z+1)={}&(2\pi)^{z/2}
 \exp\left(-\frac{z+(1+\gamma)z^2}{2}\right)\\
 &\times\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}
 \left\{\left(1+\frac zk\right)^k
 \exp\left(\frac{z^2}{2k}-z\right)\right\}.
 \end{split}
 \label{new:eq:barnes-product}
\end{equation}
For $z$ in a compact set, the logarithm of the $k$th factor after its explicit
exponential correction is
\[
 k\log\left(1+\frac zk\right)-z+\frac{z^2}{2k}
 =O(k^{-2}),
\]
uniformly.  Hence the product converges normally and defines an entire
function of $z$.

\begin{theorem}[Barnes functional equation]
The product \eqref{new:eq:barnes-product} satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \BarnesGFunction(z+1)=\Gamma(z)\BarnesGFunction(z),\qquad \BarnesGFunction(1)=1.
 \label{new:eq:barnes-functional}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for integers $n\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \BarnesGFunction(n+1)=1!\,2!\cdots(n-1)!.
 \label{new:eq:barnes-integers}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The value $\BarnesGFunction(1)=1$ follows by putting $z=0$ in the product.  Form the quotient
of \eqref{new:eq:barnes-product} at $z+1$ and $z$.  After cancellation of the
quadratic exponential normalizations and shifting one product index, the
remaining product is the reciprocal of
\[
 e^{\gamma z}\prod_{k\ge1}
 \left(1+\frac zk\right)e^{-z/k},
\]
which is $\Gamma(z+1)$ by the Weierstrass product for the gamma function.
Accounting for the elementary factor $z$ gives
$\BarnesGFunction(z+2)/\BarnesGFunction(z+1)=\Gamma(z+1)$, equivalent to
\eqref{new:eq:barnes-functional}.  Iterating from $\BarnesGFunction(1)=1$ proves the integer
formula.
\end{proof}

\section{Alexeiewsky's integral identity}

\begin{theorem}[Integral of $\log\Gamma$]
For $z$ in a simply connected domain avoiding the gamma poles and with
consistent logarithm branches,
\begin{equation}
 \log \BarnesGFunction(z+1)
 =\frac z2\log(2\pi)-\frac{z(z+1)}2
  +z\log\Gamma(z+1)
  -\int_0^z\log\Gamma(1+t)\,\Differential t.
 \label{new:eq:alexeiewsky}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the logarithm of the normally convergent product
\eqref{new:eq:barnes-product}.  Termwise differentiation gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\Differential}{\Differential z}\log \BarnesGFunction(z+1)
 &=\frac12\log(2\pi)-\frac12-(1+\gamma)z
   +\sum_{k\ge1}z\left(\frac1k-\frac1{k+z}\right)\\
 &=\frac12\log(2\pi)-\frac12-z+z\psi(z+1).
\end{aligned}
\label{new:eq:barnes-log-derivative}
\end{equation}
where the standard series
$\psi(z+1)=-\gamma+\sum_{k\ge1}(1/k-1/(k+z))$ was used.

Differentiate the right side of \eqref{new:eq:alexeiewsky}.  The derivative of
$z\log\Gamma(z+1)$ is
$\log\Gamma(z+1)+z\psi(z+1)$, and the derivative of the integral cancels the
logarithm.  The result is exactly
\eqref{new:eq:barnes-log-derivative}.  Both sides vanish at $z=0$, so they are
equal.
\end{proof}

\section{Asymptotic expansion}

\begin{theorem}[Barnes asymptotic expansion]
Uniformly in sectors $|\arg z|\le\pi-\delta$, for every fixed $p\ge1$,
\begin{align}
 \log \BarnesGFunction(z+1)
 ={}&\frac{z^2}{2}\log z-\frac{3z^2}{4}
 +\frac z2\log(2\pi)-\frac1{12}\log z+\RiemannZeta'(-1)
 \notag\\
 &+\sum_{r=1}^{p-1}
   \frac{\beta_{2r+2}}{2r(2r+2)z^{2r}}
 +O_{p,\delta}(z^{-2p}).
 \label{new:eq:barnes-asymptotic}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Insert the Euler--Maclaurin expansion of $\log\Gamma(z+1)$ into
\eqref{new:eq:alexeiewsky} and integrate term by term.  The leading terms
integrate as
\[
 \int t\log t\,\Differential t=\frac{t^2}{2}\log t-\frac{t^2}{4},
 \qquad
 \int\log t\,\Differential t=t\log t-t.
\]
After cancellation with the explicit quadratic and gamma terms in
\eqref{new:eq:alexeiewsky}, the displayed polynomial and logarithmic terms
remain.  A term
$\beta_{2r}/(2r(2r-1)t^{2r-1})$ in $\log\Gamma$ contributes, after the
cancellations, the term indexed by $r-1$ in the sum above.  The integrated
Euler--Maclaurin remainder is $O(z^{-2p})$ uniformly in the stated sectors.

The integration leaves one constant.  Evaluate along positive integers and use
$\BarnesGFunction(n+1)=\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}k!$; Euler--Maclaurin applied to the resulting double
sum identifies the constant as $\RiemannZeta'(-1)$.  Equivalently, defining the
Glaisher--Kinkelin constant $A$ by
\begin{equation}
 A=\lim_{n\to\infty}
 \frac{\prod_{k=1}^n k^k}
 {n^{n^2/2+n/2+1/12}e^{-n^2/4}},
 \label{new:eq:glaisher-definition}
\end{equation}
one obtains $\RiemannZeta'(-1)=1/12-\log A$.
\end{proof}

\section{Hyperfactorials}

Let $H_n^*=\prod_{k=1}^n k^k$.  Counting the exponent of each integer in a
triangular product gives
\begin{equation}
 H_n^*=\frac{(n!)^n}{\BarnesGFunction(n+1)}.
 \label{new:eq:hyperfactorial-barnes}
\end{equation}
Indeed, $(n!)^n$ contains $k$ to exponent $n$, whereas $\BarnesGFunction(n+1)=1!2!\cdots
(n-1)!$ contains it to exponent $n-k$.  Combining Stirling's formula with
\eqref{new:eq:barnes-asymptotic} yields
\begin{equation}
 H_n^*\sim
 A\,n^{n^2/2+n/2+1/12}e^{-n^2/4}.
 \label{new:eq:hyperfactorial-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
Thus the constant in the Barnes asymptotic is exactly the normalization
constant governing the hyperfactorial.

\chapter{Darboux's principle and coefficient asymptotics}
\label{ch:darboux}

The coefficient of a power series is a Fourier coefficient on a circle.  If a
function is smooth on that circle, repeated integration by parts makes its
coefficients small.  If smoothness fails at an isolated algebraic singularity,
the local singular terms dominate.  This is Darboux's principle in a form
suited to the inversion problems of this monograph.

\section{Exact algebraic-singularity coefficients}

For $\lambda\notin\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$,
\begin{equation}
 [z^n]\left(1-\frac z\rho\right)^{\lambda}
 =\rho^{-n}(-1)^n\binom\lambda n
 =\rho^{-n}\frac{\Gamma(n-\lambda)}
  {\Gamma(-\lambda)\Gamma(n+1)}.
 \label{new:eq:algebraic-coefficient}
\end{equation}
The gamma-ratio expansion, obtained from Euler--Maclaurin applied to
$\log\Gamma(n-\lambda)-\log\Gamma(n+1)$, gives
\begin{align}
 [z^n]\left(1-\frac z\rho\right)^{\lambda}
 ={}&\frac{\rho^{-n}n^{-\lambda-1}}{\Gamma(-\lambda)}
 \left(1+\frac{\lambda(\lambda+1)}{2n}\right.
 \notag\\[-1mm]
 &\left.\qquad
 +\frac{\lambda(\lambda+1)(\lambda+2)(3\lambda+1)}{24n^2}
 +O(n^{-3})\right).
 \label{new:eq:algebraic-coefficient-asymptotic}
\end{align}
For a pole $(1-z/\rho)^{-\alpha}$, set $\lambda=-\alpha$.

\section{A finite-smoothness Darboux theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Darboux subtraction principle]
Suppose $F$ is analytic in $|z|<\rho$ and can be written near the boundary as
\begin{equation}
 F(z)=\sum_{j=1}^Jc_j\left(1-\frac z\rho\right)^{\lambda_j}+R(z),
 \label{new:eq:darboux-decomposition}
\end{equation}
where branches are fixed near $z=\rho$.  Assume that the boundary function
$r(\theta)=R(\rho e^{i\theta})$ extends to a $2\pi$-periodic function whose
$p$th derivative is integrable and whose lower derivatives match at the
endpoints.  Then
\begin{equation}
 [z^n]F(z)
 =\sum_{j=1}^Jc_j\rho^{-n}
   \frac{\Gamma(n-\lambda_j)}
        {\Gamma(-\lambda_j)\Gamma(n+1)}
 +O(\rho^{-n}n^{-p}).
 \label{new:eq:darboux-theorem}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The singular terms have the exact coefficients
\eqref{new:eq:algebraic-coefficient}.  For the remainder, Cauchy's coefficient
formula on the circle gives
\[
 [z^n]R(z)=\frac{\rho^{-n}}{2\pi}
 \int_{-\pi}^{\pi}r(\theta)e^{-in\theta}\,\Differential\theta.
\]
Integrate by parts $p$ times.  The endpoint terms vanish because the derivatives
match periodically, and therefore
\[
 [z^n]R(z)=\frac{\rho^{-n}}{2\pi(in)^p}
 \int_{-\pi}^{\pi}r^{(p)}(\theta)e^{-in\theta}\,\Differential\theta.
\]
The integral is bounded by its $L^1$ norm, proving the stated error.
\end{proof}

The theorem says precisely what ``the nearest singularity controls the
coefficients'' means: subtract enough terms of the local singular expansion so
that the boundary remainder has enough derivatives, and the rest is a smaller
Fourier coefficient.

\section{Catalan numbers revisited}

The Catalan generating function
\[
 C(z)=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4z}}{2z}=\frac{2}{1+\sqrt{1-4z}}
\]
has its first singularity at $\rho=1/4$.  Its local expansion is
\[
 C(z)=2-2(1-4z)^{1/2}+2(1-4z)-2(1-4z)^{3/2}+\cdots.
\]
Integer powers contribute only finitely many coefficients, while the
half-integer powers control the tail.  Darboux's theorem gives the leading term
\[
 \CatalanNumber n\sim\frac{4^n}{\sqrt\pi\,n^{3/2}}.
\]
Combining the exact formula $\CatalanNumber n=(n+1)^{-1}\binom{2n}{n}$ with Stirling's
expansion refines this to
\begin{equation}
 \CatalanNumber n=\frac{4^n}{\sqrt\pi\,n^{3/2}}
 \left(1-\frac9{8n}+\frac{145}{128n^2}+O(n^{-3})\right).
 \label{new:eq:catalan-full-asymptotic}
\end{equation}

\section{Square-root singularities of inverse functions}

\begin{theorem}[Generic inverse singularity]
Let $f$ be analytic near $\tau$, and suppose
\[
 f(\tau)=\rho,\qquad f'(\tau)=0,\qquad f''(\tau)\ne0.
\]
Then every local inverse branch meeting $\tau$ has a Puiseux expansion
\begin{equation}
 g(z)=\tau-c\left(1-\frac z\rho\right)^{1/2}
       +O\left(1-\frac z\rho\right),
 \qquad
 c^2=-\frac{2\rho}{f''(\tau)},
 \label{new:eq:inverse-square-root}
\end{equation}
with the sign of $c$ determined by the branch.  If $\rho$ is the unique
dominant singularity and the remaining boundary is sufficiently smooth, then
\begin{equation}
 [z^n]g(z)\sim
 \frac{c}{2\sqrt\pi}\rho^{-n}n^{-3/2}.
 \label{new:eq:inverse-square-root-coefficient}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Taylor expansion at $\tau$ gives
\[
 z-\rho=\frac{f''(\tau)}2(w-\tau)^2+O((w-\tau)^3).
\]
Solving first for $(w-\tau)^2$ and then recursively for $w-\tau$ in powers of
$(1-z/\rho)^{1/2}$ proves the Puiseux expansion and the value of $c^2$.  The
coefficient of the leading singular term satisfies
\[
 -c[z^n](1-z/\rho)^{1/2}
 \sim\frac{c}{2\sqrt\pi}\rho^{-n}n^{-3/2},
\]
because $\Gamma(-1/2)=-2\sqrt\pi$.  Darboux subtraction makes the remaining
terms lower order.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Rooted labelled trees and Lambert $W$]
The tree function $T(z)$ satisfies $T=ze^T$, so $z=f(T)=Te^{-T}$.  Its critical
point is $\tau=1$, with $\rho=e^{-1}$ and $f''(1)=-e^{-1}=-\rho$.  Hence
$c=\sqrt2$ on the branch with $T(z)<1$ for $0<z<e^{-1}$.  Since
$[z^n]T(z)=n^{n-1}/n!$,
\[
 \frac{n^{n-1}}{n!}
 \sim\frac{e^n}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,n^{3/2}},
\]
which is also immediate from Stirling's formula.  The singularity analysis
explains why the universal exponent $n^{-3/2}$ appears in tree enumeration.
\end{example}

\chapter{A unified coefficient algorithm layer}
\label{ch:algorithm-layer-new}

The formulas in this monograph are constructive.  This chapter collects
implementation-neutral recurrences that compute the main transforms without
symbolic expansion of large powers.  They also serve as independent
certificates for tables and examples.

\section{Arithmetic with ordinary series}

Let $A(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_nz^n$ with $a_0$ invertible.

\paragraph{Reciprocal.}
For $C=1/A=\sum c_nz^n$,
\begin{equation}
 c_0=a_0^{-1},\qquad
 c_n=-a_0^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^na_jc_{n-j}.
 \label{new:eq:series-reciprocal-algorithm}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Quotient.}
For $C=B/A$, with $B=\sum b_nz^n$,
\begin{equation}
 c_n=a_0^{-1}\left(b_n-\sum_{j=1}^na_jc_{n-j}\right).
 \label{new:eq:series-quotient-algorithm}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Logarithm and exponential.}
Assume $a_0=1$.  If $L=\log A=\sum_{n\ge1}\ell_nz^n$, then $A'=AL'$ gives
\begin{equation}
 \ell_n=a_n-\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}j\ell_j a_{n-j}.
 \label{new:eq:series-log-algorithm}
\end{equation}
Conversely, if $A=e^L$,
\begin{equation}
 a_0=1,\qquad
 a_n=\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n j\ell_j a_{n-j}.
 \label{new:eq:series-exp-algorithm}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Arbitrary powers.}
For $C=A^\alpha$, logarithmic differentiation $AC'=\alpha A'C$ yields
\begin{equation}
 c_0=1,\qquad
 c_n=\frac1n\sum_{j=1}^n\bigl((\alpha+1)j-n\bigr)a_jc_{n-j}.
 \label{new:eq:series-power-algorithm}
\end{equation}
Each recurrence follows by equating the coefficient of $z^n$ in the displayed
defining identity, so it is valid over any characteristic-zero coefficient
ring.

\section{Reversion by triangular recursion}

Let $F(z)=a_1z+a_2z^2+\cdots$ with $a_1$ invertible, and let
$G(z)=b_1z+b_2z^2+\cdots$ satisfy $F(G(z))=z$.  Then $b_1=a_1^{-1}$ and, for
$n\ge2$,
\begin{equation}
 b_n=-a_1^{-1}\sum_{k=2}^n a_k[z^n]G(z)^k.
 \label{new:eq:reversion-triangular}
\end{equation}
The coefficient on the right depends only on $b_1,\ldots,b_{n-1}$, so this is a
triangular algorithm.  Ordinary partial Bell polynomials compute the inner
coefficient efficiently:
\[
 [z^n]G(z)^k=\OrdinaryPartialBellPolynomial nk(b_1,b_2,\ldots).
\]
Lagrange inversion gives a nonrecursive formula; the triangular recurrence is
often preferable for exact computation because every step can be checked by a
single composition coefficient.

\section{Good inversion by truncated multivariate arithmetic}
